- **A scan for meta-narration — *this chapter, this volume, this batch, this project, the reader, this account, the manuscript, the outline*, and a chapter number in the prose — returns ZERO in the prose of Chapters 221–230. It returns the ten H1 headings, which are the house heading format and are not prose.** **THREE INSTANCES WERE IN THE FIRST WRITING AND ALL THREE WERE TAKEN OUT BEFORE DELIVERY: *it is the reason this chapter exists* at `225:5`, *it is the only time in this batch that a man of thirty-one says anything about himself that is a defence* at `225:35`, and *it is the only time in this batch that anybody in this basin hears about her* at `226:73`.** The review of Batch 0002 found nine of this class in Batch 0002 and recorded ten in Batch 0001 unrepaired, and the cause is the same cause it named: **the chapter cards are written in the frame *the finding is…* and the prose copies the card's frame instead of the scene.** This batch's cards were written in the frame *the finding is* three times and the prose caught it three times. **A Batch 0004 writer is told here that the manuscript contains ten unrepaired instances in Chapters 201 to 210, of which `205:48` names a chapter number in the prose, and is told not to build on the frame and not to copy the phrasing. AND THE SCOPE OF THIS SWEEP IS STATED BECAUSE A SWEEP THAT REPORTS ZERO IS A FACT ABOUT A RANGE AND NOT ABOUT THE BOOK: this one covered Chapters 221 to 230 only, it is silent about the ten in 201 to 210, and there is one more in 207 that no sweep has caught, which is the word *reader* inside the panel at `207:67`. See section 14, item 12.**
- **Two block-quoted physical documents in two distinct documents, and they are the batch's only two: the draft of a remedy, nine lines including a head, at `226:9`–`17`, and the sheet as eleven people left it, eight lines including a head, at `228:13`–`20`.** Every one is a physical object somebody is holding — the first flat on a table in the Seat's one room and then lifted off it, the second in the hands of a man of thirty-four in a road — **and there is no third category and no third document.** **The first writing of `226:10` carried a markdown strikethrough, `~~…~~`, which is a fourth formatting category, and it was taken out before delivery and the strike is now carried by the document's own head and by the prose.** The list of nineteen answers is not restaged and not block-quoted; the four pages are not block-quoted again; the water-book is not block-quoted again.
- **The word NINE stands EIGHTY-FOUR TIMES over Chapters 221–230**, case-insensitively, and that is a count of occurrences of the word and not of anything in the story. **It stood at eighty-eight before the review repair of 26 September 2026, and four of the eight went in that repair and all four were false counts of words: *about nine words* at `222:23`, `223:27` and `225:5`, and *eleven words* at `225:37`. The review of this batch counted the same ten chapters twice and reported the figure as having held flat against Batch 0002, and it did not hold flat: Batch 0002 stands at eighty-eight over ten chapters of the same length and this batch now stands four below it, and the direction of the change is the right one.** **Batch 0001's reviewed run stands at ninety-eight over ten chapters, so the three batches of this volume read ninety-eight, eighty-eight and eighty-four.** The load-bearing nines in this batch and the reason each stands: the nine pounds a roof; the nine shillings a week; the nine days' ride to Orison; nine times in eleven days and this was the tenth; the ninth door on the walk; the ninth turn; about nine people in a lane or on a bank or in a room; nine days of a sheet in a cart shed; and the nine words of the refusals that are estimates and not counts. Everything else was taken out, and four more went in the repair.
- **The word ELEVEN stands SIXTY-FOUR TIMES over Chapters 221–230**, and that is a count of occurrences and not of anything in the story, and **it is a number this volume has been carrying for four batches and this is the batch in which it is the subject. IT STOOD AT SIXTY-FIVE BEFORE THE REVIEW REPAIR AND THE ONE THAT WENT WAS A FALSE COUNT AT `225:37`, and the review reported the figure as having held flat against Batch 0002's sixty-five. It did not hold flat in either direction: Batch 0002 stands at ONE HUNDRED AND TWENTY-FIVE over the same ten chapters of the same length, because that is the flood batch and the flood batch is the batch of eleven feet and eleven houses, and this batch is sixty below it.** The load-bearing elevens: the eleven houses, the eleven marks, the eleven feet, the eleven seats, the eleven men at the pit, the eleven shillings a week, the eleven hundred tons, the eleven years, the eleven days, the eleven days a sluice opens early, the eleven words, and **the eleven hands**, which is the batch. **TWO OF THE SIXTY-FIVE WERE CUT AS FILLER BEFORE DELIVERY: *about eleven minutes* at `224:3`, which is about ten, and *about eleven hours* at `227:3`, which was an echo of the eleven hands in the same paragraph and is now *the length of a working day*.** `227:62` is the volume's own joke about the number said out loud in a room, in a clerk's mouth, and it is the sentence that makes the rest of the sixty-five defensible.
- **The stock pause *a minute and a half* returns ZERO over Chapters 221–230.** It stood once in the whole of Volume 05, at `209:25`, and **two of them were in the first writing of this batch, at `221:19` and `227:39`, and both were taken out before delivery** — the first became *the time it takes to get a boot off* and the second became *as long as it takes to grind ink*, which the volume uses elsewhere and which is the right measure for a man deciding whether to say a thing. **The repair took one of the four surviving *grind ink* pauses out, at `223:9`, and it became *as long as it takes to drink a cup of something*, which the volume also uses elsewhere, and three of the four stand at `226:19`, `227:39` and `228:36`.**
- **AND THE MECHANICAL LAYER, measured after the repair: *about nine words* stands at SIX over the ten chapters where it stood at ten, *about four seconds* at five, *is not a thing you can get* at five, and twenty-three paragraphs open with *Nobody*, which is two hundred and twenty-seven fewer than nothing and is four fewer than Batch 0002's twenty-seven. *About nine words* is a device of the whole project and not of this batch — Batch 0002 used it once and `state/character-state.md` records it against eleven characters across four volumes — and what the repair removed was not the device but four instances of it standing next to a quoted sentence whose real length was not the number printed.**

### 3. THE THREE CLOCKS, unchanged, obeyed in every chapter, and the engine of the volume is still arithmetic and not politics

**The Shelf keeps a grain year of fifty-two weeks. The Red Basin keeps three clocks and there is no conversion between any of them, and the finding that there is no conversion was made ONCE, at `202:45`, in a salt-pan's mouth, and forty-eight chapters are about what people do about a thing that has already been found out.** The pan-book year: fifty saltings, more or less, a salting being a drying, a dry-year salting about seven weeks and a wet-year one eleven, and therefore no length in a salting and no first drying that holds twice. The turn: a year from the first shaking, which is turn nought, twelve years back, and this year is turn twelve. The day: a day since the second shaking, out of the water-book, and it is the only date in this basin that can be put on the outside of a bag that is going nine days to a city of four hundred thousand people.

**AND THIS BATCH PUT THE BASIN'S DAY-COUNT ON A PIECE OF PAPER ELEVEN OTHER PEOPLE WROTE, for the first time in the volume, at `225:59`, and the reason is the second refusal of a man of sixty-one.** Fen Tregerran, who has kept a notched board for thirty years and whose count has never been out, was asked to date the settlement and refused in about nine words: **his count is a count of dryings and the paper is about water, and a paper about water dated in a count of dryings is the two rows that do not meet, and he has watched that go wrong twice in thirty years and once of them was his own mother's house.** **HE PUT HIS HAND TO THE PAPER ANYWAY, and said afterward that a hand and a date are two different acts, and that if anybody in that room could not tell the difference between a hand and a date then the room was in the wrong trade.** The date is therefore the water-book's, a hundred and seventy-three, written in a man's own hand because the only man in the basin who could have dated it in his own count would not. **That is the volume's engine turning over in a practical document and it is the whole of what the three clocks have been for.**

### 4. THE MONEY, every figure recomputed in a script and the working printed beside it. **THE INSTRUMENT IS THE SAME ONE EVERY TIME: print the unit price in the sentence, find it, and multiply.**

- **A matter carried to the chamber of plenty at Orison and brought back is £5 6s** — entry a pound, the day four pounds, nine days' board at eightpence. 240d + 960d + 72d = **1,272d = £5 6s** ✓ (`226:7`, `229:41`). **It is named twice in this batch and it is NOT SPENT, and the not spending of it is the batch's second refusal by a different person for a different reason (`229:41`).**
- **Ilma Treen: nine shillings a week for the sixteen weeks the Sallow runs = £7 4s a year, and nothing the rest of the year.** 16 × 108d = **1,728d = £7 4s** ✓. **THE WHOLE OF IT, and not the half of it, is now paid by the Anner, so the Anner's outlay on the wage is 1,728d a year and not the 864d it already paid, and the sheet says *the whole of the above* and not *the first half*, and that change of words is the batch's money in one sentence.**
- **The arrears: the pit paid 4s 6d of the nine and the Seat owed 4s 6d; sixteen weeks at 4s 6d is £3 12s and three years of it is £10 16s.** 16 × 54d = 864d = £3 12s ✓; 3 × 864d = 2,592d = **£10 16s** ✓. **Paid in one week, in an envelope, at the closing of the water, in about ten minutes (`224:3`, `224:7`).**
- **A sluice gate: the frame £4 10s and the oak £1 8s, together £5 18s, and the whole of what anybody in this basin has spent on that structure in three years is now a gate that works, and it is hung in Chapter 230 and the lath with the figure on it goes in a cart shed.** 1,080d + 336d = **1,416d = £5 18s** ✓.
- **A roof, wet through, is about £9 to put back, and it is NOT broken into thatch and battens and nails, and the man who does roofs is asked to split it three times in this batch and refuses three times and says why the second time and never says it again (`217:23`, `223:23`, `224:37`, `224:47`).** £9 = **2,160d** ✓. **AND THE CHAPTER AS DELIVERED PRINTED 216d TWICE AT `223:15` AND SAID THAT 216 TIMES NINETEEN IS 41,040d, and the review of this batch caught it, and the line now reads two thousand one hundred and sixty pence twice and its conclusion is unchanged at a hundred and seventy-one pounds with nothing over. THIS PARAGRAPH HAD CERTIFIED THE CANON FIGURE AND HAD NEVER READ THE SENTENCE THAT PRINTS IT, and that is a defect in the way this section is written and not only in the chapter: a money section that re-derives its own totals and never reads the prose cannot catch a chapter, and the standing instruction — print the unit price in the sentence, find it, and multiply — is a sentence-level instruction that this section ran against itself. A money section that cites a line number is not thereby a check of that line.**
- **Nineteen houses at about £9 a roof is a hundred and seventy-one pounds.** 2,160d × 19 = 41,040d; 41,040 ÷ 240 = **171 with no remainder** ✓ (`223:19`).
- **THE SECOND HAND OF THE WATER-BOOK, at a penny a day for every day the water runs: sixteen weeks of seven days is 112 days, and 112d divided by twelve is nine shillings and fourpence with nothing over** (`224:17`, `224:23`). **9s 4d is about a fifteenth of the sluice-keeper's whole paid year: 1,728 ÷ 112 = 15.4** (`224:23`).
- **THE RECURRING FIGURE, and this is the batch's central money, and the first writing of this batch got it WRONG and the working won and it is recorded here rather than hidden: roofs 41,040d + the whole wage 1,728d + the second hand 112d = 42,880d = £178 13s 4d a year.** Check: 42,880 − 42,720 = 160d; 160 ÷ 12 = 13s 4d ✓. **THE FIRST WRITING SAID £171 16s 8d, WHICH WAS THE ROOFS PLUS AN AMOUNT THAT IS NOT A WAGE: it added 88d, which is not a figure in this basin. The outline's table was corrected at the site, with the working printed and the working named as the winner, before Chapter 224 was written, and the chapter was corrected before delivery, and the two are the same correction and the prose is the fact.** 42,880d ÷ 1,848,000d = **0.0232, and the chapter says two and three-tenths in the hundred** (`223:41`).
- **THE ONE-OFFS: the gate £5 18s and the arrears £10 16s together £16 14s.** 1,416d + 2,592d = 4,008d; 4,008 ÷ 240 = 16 remainder 168d; 168 ÷ 12 = 14s ✓ (`223:43`).
- **THE FIRST YEAR OF IT, ALL OF IT: £195 7s 4d.** 42,880d + 4,008d = 46,888d; 46,888 − 46,800 = 88d = 7s 4d ✓ (`229:21`).
- **AND THE FINDING, printed with its own subtraction in a lane in front of about forty people: the first year of the settlement costs £24 7s 4d MORE than the flood did.** 46,888d − 41,040d = 5,848d; 5,848 ÷ 240 = 24 remainder 88d = 7s 4d ✓ (`229:23`).
- **And the excess in the only unit the lane has: £24 7s 4d is 5,848d, and a sluice-keeper's whole paid year is 1,728d, and 5,848 ÷ 1,728 = 3.38, so the paper costs three years and about six weeks more than the only wage anybody in that village has ever been paid.** 3 × 1,728 = 5,184; 5,848 − 5,184 = 664d; 664 ÷ 108d = 6.15 ✓ (`229:25`). **AND IT IS A HAIR UNDER ONE PART IN EIGHT OF THE WHOLE THING THAT IS NOT ROOFS: 5,848 × 8 = 46,784, and 46,888 − 46,784 = 104d, so it is just under one in eight** (`229:25`).
- **The paper: two pages at a penny and a sealed copy at threepence is five pence, and the woman who copies it is not paid for it and says so in about nine words (`226:23`).** 2d + 3d = **5d** ✓.
- **A hundredweight of salt at the pans is £1 4s, and red ochre is £4 a ton at the pit's mouth and £11 a ton at Sallowby, and the Anner's difference is £7,700 a year** (`223:11`). 1,100 × 4 = 4,400; 1,100 × 11 = 12,100; 1,100 × 7 = **7,700** ✓. **THE FORTY-FIVE TIMES COMPARISON AT `219:13` IS NOT RESTAGED AND THE FIGURE FORTY-FIVE APPEARS NOWHERE IN THIS BATCH; the percentage is used instead, because a clerk's percentage is a thing anybody in that room can check and a forty-fifth is not.**
- **AND THE FIGURES THAT TRAVEL AS DEBTS AND NOT AS PRICES, AND NONE OF THEM IS NAMED IN THIS BATCH, WHICH IS A DECISION AND NOT AN OMISSION.** Four hundred and eighty-five pounds a year on nine thousand seven hundred at the close of that grain year; the second notice of five shillings and fourpence; the claim of forty-five pounds two shillings and eightpence to a carter of fifty-one with a boy, eleven miles off, not producible, no form for it, and he is right; the ninety-one numbered sheets; the box of about nine hundred and forty forms; the minute of the first of the forty-eighth week; the dead man in the fourth line of a list of two hundred and forty and his wife. Check: 9,700 × 12d = 116,400d = £485 ✓; 4 × 16d = 64d = 5s 4d ✓; 406 × 32d − 2,160d = 10,832d = £45 2s 8d ✓. **ALL SIX ARE NAMED AT `230:35` AND FOUR HUNDRED AND EIGHTY-FIVE POUNDS IS THE ONLY ONE OF THE SIX THAT PUTS A FIGURE IN A RED BASIN'S MOUTH, and it is put there in a list of things that are not stopping, which is what the Shelf half of a book is for and what the basin half of that book is not.**

### 5. WHAT THE BATCH DELIVERED, once each, with the chapter and the line

1. **A LIST OF WHAT NINETEEN PEOPLE NEED IS NOT A LIST OF WORK, and it took a morning, and eighteen of the nineteen answers cannot be bought and the one that can is a roof, and the order of the work is bed, boards, salt, and then the roof, and a woman of about sixty said *the roof is second* and was right, and nobody has written her name down anywhere because nothing else happened to her that week** (`221:27`, `221:31`).
2. **A MAN MAY PUT A DAY ON HIS OWN ACT AND MAY NOT PUT ONE ON ANOTHER PERSON'S SPEECH, in his own hand, as the second line under the eleventh day-count of a penny exercise book, and there are nineteen people in that lane who have not been asked for a day by anybody in their lives** (`221:11`).
3. **A LIST OF ANSWERS WITH NO ADDRESSES ON IT IS NINETEEN OPINIONS, AND A LIST WITH ADDRESSES ON IT CAN BE ARGUED AGAINST THE WRONG DOOR IN A PERSON'S OWN FRONT DOOR, and it was found at the ninth door of a walk that took four hours and about two hundred yards of lane, and the ninth answer was struck through and NOT taken off the sheet** (`222:43`).
4. **A LIST OF HOUSES IS A LIST OF PEOPLE, AND THE DAY THERE IS A LIST OF PEOPLE THE NEXT HOUSE IS A HOUSE THAT IS NOT ON THE LIST, said by an under-manager in about a minute at the top of a lane, and it is the reason a settlement of work was possible and the reason nobody in this basin can say whether a house put up after this year is inside the line or in front of it** (`222:7`).
5. **LET IT ALL BE THE ANNER, said in about six minutes by a man who had been waiting six days for somebody to come up the hill and ask him a question about money, and a mine that pays a wage once a week is a different kind of mine than the one he has been under-manager of since he was thirty-three, and he is not saying that is a reason to refuse** (`223:11`).
6. **WHAT THE ANNER CAN PAY IS NOTHING. £178 13s 4d out of the £7,700 a year that is the whole of what has been argued about in this basin is two and three-tenths in the hundred, and a man in a good coat would call that nothing, and it took three days to establish and it is the finding** (`223:41`).
7. **IT IS NOT THE FOUR AND SIX. A woman of thirty-eight said in nine words that the Seat did not fail to pay her, because nobody promised her the other four and six, and that if the paper says *not by the Seat* then in a year somebody will ask the Seat why it stopped and the Seat will say it never started and both will be right, and she is the only person in the room who will know which of the two is a lie** (`226:33`).
8. **THE FIRST MONEY IN THIS BASIN EVER PAID ANYBODY FOR LOOKING AT WATER is nine shillings and fourpence a year, and the girl of seventeen who keeps the second hand named the figure herself and said it twice and had it checked once by a man who counts carts, and the woman who wrote it down said out loud that she was not going to improve it** (`224:17`, `224:23`).
9. **A BOOK OF A RIVER KEPT BY FOUR PEOPLE WHO HAVE NOT STOOD AT THE FRAME KEEPS WHAT THEY WERE TOLD, and on the third night of last week the mill hand and the Seat's hand disagree about that frame by about two hours and both of them were in the room and neither of them went up the hill, and the difference between those two hours is the whole of what happened in that lane** (`224:37`).
10. **A MAN WHO LOST A HOUSE IS NOT A HOUSE, said by a man of thirty-four in a lane on the fifth day of that week, and the sentence that changed the shape of the paper is EIGHTEEN WORDS long and it is his — *a paper with a man's name on it is read out in a year when he is gone* — and it is the reason nineteen doors and nineteen numbers came off a piece of paper and four lines about work and days went on** (`225:37`, `225:39`, `225:49`). **This item said *in eleven words* and said *in a room* and both were wrong: the count was wrong by seven and the chapter says he said it to a lane and not to a room.**
11. **A PAPER WITH A MAN'S NAME ON IT IS READ OUT IN A YEAR WHEN HE IS GONE, in eighteen words, at the bottom of a lane, and it is the reason the name came out, and he said they were right and did not put it back, and the space at the foot was not filled and a clerk of forty-four refused to put a dash in it because a dash is a thing people put where a name used to be so that a later person thinks nothing was ever there** (`225:37`, `227:37`).
12. **THE NINETEEN ROOFS ARE THE ONLY LINE OUT OF FOUR WITH A FIGURE ON IT, and the settlement of this valley is therefore a settlement about one line out of four and about the order the other three are done in, and nobody in that lane said that out loud for about two days** (`221:35`).
13. **HIS VERSION, BLOCK-QUOTED, DOCUMENT 1: seven lines, a struck first line left on the paper, and three words under a name at the foot** (`226:9`–`17`). **And five objections, and every one of them was made by a person the draft names or does not name, and not one of them was made by a man from four hundred miles off** (`226:21`).
14. **A SCRIVENER OF THIRTY-THREE WHO COPIED IT FOR NOTHING ASKED THAT A STRUCK LINE NOT BE LEFT ON A PIECE OF PAPER, because anybody who comes in that room in a year will spend a month working out who took it back and why and will not get it right, because it was not a clerk who took it back** (`226:25`).
15. **THE UNDER-MANAGER ASKED FOR THE WORD *ASKED*. Not that the Anner shall pay again. Asked. He wanted it read out four times and he wanted his own hand on that line and not a clerk's, and he wrote it in ochre dust, and the two hands on that line are the only two on the sheet anybody in the room can tell apart, and a clerk called that a defect and refused to write it down** (`226:43`).
16. **THE WOMAN OF ABOUT FORTY SAID, IN NINE WORDS, THAT IF HE PUT A NAME AT THE FOOT OF THAT PAPER IT WAS A PAPER ABOUT A MAN FROM FOUR HUNDRED MILES OFF, and he agreed with her before she had finished and said *take it out*, and then she refused to let it be easy, and he said the whole reason in a lane in front of about eleven people, and nobody asked him for a reason** (`227:19`, `227:27`, `227:41`).
17. **THE BATCH'S MIDPOINT. THE REMEDY IS REWRITTEN IN ELEVEN HANDS THAT ARE NOT HIS AND HE IS NOT ONE OF THEM, and the eleven hours were not in a room, and the eleven people were asked one at a time in eleven places, and four of them who could be found together did not sign in front of each other** (`227:3`, `227:5`).
18. **THE PANEL, AND ITS SUBJECT IS THE FINDING AND NOT A RULE ANYBODY IN THIS BASIN HAS EVER HEARD OF: the six lines name six things and the eleven hands are on them, and the part of the sheet nobody will agree on is the part that says which houses are under the line; and WHAT IS NOT DECIDED is whether a house put up after a line is drawn is inside the line or in front of it, and whether anyone in this basin will ever be the one to say. It took nothing out of him, it answered nothing, and nobody in that lane saw anything** (`227:53`–`54`).
19. **THERE ARE ELEVEN HANDS ON THAT SHEET AND IT IS NOT THE ELEVEN HOUSES AND IT IS NOT THE ELEVEN MARKS AND IT IS NOT THE ELEVEN FEET AND IT IS NOT THE ELEVEN SEATS IN THIS ROOM AND IT IS NOT THE ELEVEN MEN AT THE PIT, and a clerk of forty-four said it out loud unasked because he had watched six people go quiet, and about four wrote it down in their own books and about six did not** (`227:62`).
20. **THEIR VERSION, BLOCK-QUOTED, DOCUMENT 2: six lines, one hand, and three hands on a struck line that do not agree, printed on the face of the paper because a clerk of forty-four wrote a sentence about it at somebody else's request and read it twice** (`228:13`–`20`).
21. **THE ORDER'S FOURTH LINE HAS SAID SINCE THE HUNDRED AND FIFTY-FIFTH DAY THAT A COPY IS TO BE KEPT IN THE LANE BY A PERSON OF THE LANE, AND NOBODY HAD EVER ASKED WHAT HAPPENS TO A COPY THAT IS KEPT BY A MAN WHO CANNOT READ IT, AND IT IS READ ALOUD IN A ROAD IN FRONT OF ABOUT FORTY PEOPLE, and that is the whole of why, and a man of thirty-four reads his own settlement aloud and changes two words while he is saying it and nobody stops him** (`228:5`, `228:13`).
22. **HE PUT IN *AND EVERY WEEK*, after nine shillings a week, and said before he said it that six people had argued for two days about four and six and had never once written down the half of the argument, and that the half of the argument is that the money was never the trouble and the day it came on was the trouble** (`228:28`).
23. **AND SOMEONE TO COME AND LOOK AT A WALL BEFORE WINTER, which has no figure on it and is not going to get one, and the woman who took the nineteen answers down said out loud that a thing with no figure on it was wanted, and the clerk of the Seat said he had stood in about forty roads in nine years and watched a document acquire a line in one and had not been able to do one single thing about it, and that the reason is that the man who acquired it is a person of the lane** (`228:34`, `228:40`).
24. **THE SETTLEMENT COSTS MORE THAN THE FLOOD, and the excess is a hair under one part in eight of the whole thing and is not roofs, and in the only unit the lane has the excess is three years and about six weeks of the whole of a sluice-keeper's paid year, and the working was on a board in a cart shed and anybody could look at it** (`229:23`, `229:25`).
25. **NOBODY CARRIED IT TO ORISON, and a clerk of forty-four put it in about eleven words, and the reason is that the bench is seized of the matter already and would come back with two paragraphs, and the second good reason for anything anybody in that room did in a month was given to him by a mine, and about four people noticed that the second time that money was not spent was the first time in nine years a clerk of that seat had found a reason not to spend it** (`229:41`).
26. **A GATE OF FIVE POUNDS EIGHTEEN HUNG IN ONE DAY BY TWO MEN, one of whom is on ten shillings a week and was there on his own time and told nobody and was not thanked for it, and about thirty people came down a lane to watch a gate open, which is not a thing anybody has ever done in this basin, and about nine of the thirty were children and four of the nine had been under two feet of water in the week before last** (`230:3`, `230:13`).
27. **AND THE WHOLE OF WHAT THE SHEET DOES NOT SAY, which is a height. Eleven feet above the Anner's own sill is the number that put two feet of water into nineteen houses, and it is on a wall, in a bond, in a chain-book, in about nine books and on about nine doorposts, and it is not on the sheet. He had a pencil out and about four inches of space under the struck line and he did not write anything in it, and he put the pencil in his coat, and that is the whole of the last act of a man of thirty-one in this valley in the fifty-fourth week** (`230:19`).
28. **HE DID NOT PUT IT IN BECAUSE A RECORDWRIGHT SAID NINE WORDS AND WAS RIGHT, and because he is not a party, and because there is no form in this basin for a man with nothing in his hands to have a paper in front of him, and because a man who has learned a thing once in a room in another county is not entitled to say it out loud a second time in a lane in a different county to a woman of thirty-four who lost a house to about two feet of water** (`230:21`).
29. **AND THE SYSTEM SAID NOTHING, and about eleven days ago he had begun, without noticing he was doing it, to expect it to say something, and on the third of that week it became clear to him that he had stopped expecting it, and he did not tell anybody that he had stopped expecting it, and there is no instrument in this basin for anybody to notice it in him** (`230:29`).
30. **AND THE BATCH ENDS ON THREE ORDINARY THINGS AND NONE OF THEM IS A RULING: a gate opened at the closing of the water, a chair put back at a table, and a lath gone in a cart shed** (`230:41`).

### 6. THE DEVICES, and none advanced

- **The leg. CANON and carried: the finding it rests on is `155:7` — the leg goes when he is carrying a thing somebody else is going to have to read, and it comes back when the carrying stops. IN THIS BATCH IT DID NOT COME BACK, and that is a consequence of the carrying and not a rule and not a cure.** It is used ONCE, at `230:17`, on a wet clay bank at the second light, as a fact and not as a discovery, and the reason is not given and he has not told one person in this valley. **NO FILE AND NO CHAPTER IN VOLUME 05 STATES A COUNT OF DAYS SINCE THE LEG CLEARED, AND NONE MAY, and none does, and `230:33` says the carrying has cost him four hundred miles and a leg he has not got back and prints no interval and puts no arithmetic on it.**
- **The rendering. Not staged in Batch 0001, not staged in Batch 0002, not staged, referred to or hinted at here.** Volume 04's last word on it is *not to be referred to again*.
- **The left hand, the vantage, the door in the picture, the token and the afterimage: none is mentioned in this batch and none is advanced.** The token is not touched and the fourth use stands: it took the words.
- **The continuity line is not refused again and no twelfth refusal is permitted, and it is not on the Red Basin's table.** Volume 04 refused it eleven times. It is not refused here and it is not paid off.
- **THE COUNT OF THE PEOPLE WHO KNOW HE CANNOT READ IS NOT PRINTED IN THIS PROJECT, in any file, in any figure, and it is not printed in this block, and this batch adds nothing to it in the fiction.** Refer to `154:53` and `164:49` and to nothing else. **It is exercised three times in ten chapters and it is a real constraint every time and it is never a disclosure and never a count: the sheet is never once put into his hands and is in the Seat's hand or in a man's hands; a man who also cannot read reads the settlement aloud in a road and changes two words while he is saying it, and nobody in that lane works out why a man can do that; and the girl of seventeen who carries the copy down the hill is the only person in the lane who was given the basin's day-count, and she was given it and did not ask where it came from, and it is the man from four hundred miles off who gave it to her.** **A first writing of Chapter 226 said that he *cannot write a fair hand and never has been able to*, which is a new claim about him and about his handwriting rather than about the barred limit, and it was taken out before delivery and replaced with a statement about who writes at the desk in that room and about his not being a party to the sheet.**
- **AND THE TWO DEVICES THIS BATCH SPENDS ARE SPENT HERE, once each, and neither may be restaged: a document read aloud in a lane by a man who cannot read it and amended in the reading, so that a change made aloud cannot be attributed to a hand (`228`); and a piece of paper that prints its own disagreement on its face, in three hands on one struck line, with no form in this basin for a paper to say that (`227`, `228`).**

### 8. WHAT A LATER PASS MUST NOT DO IN VOLUME 05

1. **Do not answer the new question and do not compress it into one word.** It is written once, at `210:39` and `210:41`, in his own hand, in a penny exercise book in a cart shed, with a place and a form of words, and he does not ask. **It is not raised in this batch and no person in the Red Basin asks him which year it was, and he is not relieved of the not being asked.**
2. **Do not add a panel beyond the three in the volume, one at `207:67`–`68`, one at `217:35`–`36` and one at `227:53`–`54`, and do not add a day-count instance beyond the twelve in Chapters 201–230 without putting its chapter and its basin day in `outline/volume-05.md` first, and do not print a count of instances without saying it is a count of instances.**
3. **Do not put a weekday anywhere and do not put a month-name anywhere.** **The two month-dates in Batch 0003 were both in the first writing and both were taken out, and the same has happened in each of the last three batches of this volume, and the lesson is that the guardrail is not the problem and the scan is late.**
4. **Do not print a count of the people who know he cannot read, anywhere, including in a hand-off note, and do not add to it in the fiction. Refer to `154:53` and `164:49` and to nothing else.**
5. **Do not give Ilyan an office, a party, a bench, a body, a remedy he chose alone, or a stage.** He is Stage 4, arrived in Chapter 177, and it is spent. **A scan for *Remedy Drafter* over Chapters 201–230 returns zero.** **AND HIS NAME IS NOT ON THE ELEVEN-HAND SHEET AND THE PLACE WHERE IT WAS IS NOT FILLED AND NOT DASHED, and a later pass may not put it back and may not fill the space and may not have anybody put it back for him.**
6. **Do not produce a person beyond the one this batch adds and the outline names.** **THE CLASS IS DEFINED AND THE COUNT IS NOW FIVE, and Batch 0003 adds exactly one: Coll Ferren, thirty-four, four people, named at `221:41` standing in his own doorway.** The four before him are Ewan Troke (`201:19`), Fen Tregerran (`202:5`), Alo Vearn (`209:5`) and the man who comes through the north twice a year (`210:53`). **THE LINE IS DRAWN AND IT IS DRAWN BY THE OUTLINE AND NOT BY THE CHAPTER, and the honest form of it is that every other person in Chapters 221 to 230 has been in that basin for the whole of the batch or is a person named in Batch 0001 or Batch 0002, and a woman of about sixty who is not named and a woman of about thirty-one with two children and a man of thirty-five with a ladder and a pitman who hangs a gate are all people who have been in a house the whole time. A woman of about forty who washes the pen is still not named, and a man of about forty with a crowbar is not in this batch, and a man of about seventy-six is not in this batch.**
7. **Do not undo anything and do not relieve anybody.** The lease is answered and not renewed and not lost; the carrying is off nothing; the blank is a blank; the second notice is unpaid; nobody is redeemed; **nobody forgives anybody, and the batch says so in its own last paragraph; and the Anner has a sheet now, and a mine with a sheet has a standing interest in a number that put water in nineteen houses, and that interest is the batch's cost and it is not a resolution and it does not go away when the roofs are on.**
8. **Do not introduce a new enemy and do not make the world bigger.** The people who keep this volume's disputes from being settled are about thirty clerks in a building with a yard at Orison who did their jobs properly, **and in this batch one of them was given a reason not to spend five pounds and six, and the reason was supplied by a mine and not by a person, and no clerk was relieved and no clerk is redeemed.**
9. **Do not open a redacted archive and do not have anybody ask for it, AND DO NOT NAME THE FOURTH COLUMN AGAIN.** It was named once, at `218:25`, by a clerk who said he was never going to ask about it, and Batch 0002 did not name it and **Batch 0003 did not name it and did not have anybody else name it and did not ask it.** The climax is that HE opens it, in Batch 0005.
10. **Do not let Sable Venn arrive, negotiate, or make an offer.** She was named once from a distance in Chapter 210 and one person in that room repeated the name wrong. **Batch 0002 did not name her and Batch 0003 did not name her.**
11. **Do not reach the stone and do not let anybody stand in front of it.** Anchor 8 remains missing beneath the Red Basin until Volume 14. **The second half of the volume's question is not asked out loud in any chapter of this batch and is not even a whisper, as a panel, as a rendering, as a voice, or as a person remarking that anything answered, and `230:29` says in its own words that the System said nothing and that a man has stopped expecting it to.**
12. **Do not restore the right of refusal, answer the historical question, advance the authorship question or the Shale Mirror, name NK-3317, call for Tamsin, put Neris Ro or Bram Orren in a scene, answer the Bridgeward, send anybody to Carrisand, redeem Ansa Rell, or ask about Alma Gannock's boy of eight. Do not name the First Witness. Do not make Ors Halm the last problem in the series.**
13. **Do not restate the sentence at `156:63` and do not relieve him of it.** The four words were quoted exactly once afterwards, at `194:43`, and may not be quoted again, explained, or put in anybody's mouth. **What happened in this batch is that a category was reached again by ordinary people, about documents, in a second medium, and in the plainest possible form, and that the man who reached it did NOT say it out loud, and that the reason he did not is printed in the chapter and is that a recordwright said nine words and was right and that he is not a party.** **He reached it at `230:19` and `230:21` and wrote it in his own book in four words and did not say it in a room.**
14. **Do not use the 179 and 189 shape a third time.** A scan for uppercase CORRECT returns zero over Chapters 201–230.
15. **Do not restage the eight devices Volume 05 spends, and each has been used once and none may be used again: a word defined twice in two margins (201); a deed in two pieces (203); the two-calendars-do-not-convert finding, said once in a salt-pan's mouth (202); the quick ruling's four complete lines as a discovery (207); the mark that is not a measurement (213); the order's own logic examined (217); a document read aloud by a man who cannot read it and amended in the reading (228); and a paper that prints its own disagreement on its face (227, 228).**

**`state/phase-ledger.json` is controller-owned and is three volumes stale: it reads `currentPhase: batch-0002`, Volume 01, Chapters 11–20, status `planned`, attempts 0. NOTHING READS THAT FILE — `grep -rn "phase-ledger" scripts/ .github/` returns nothing — and `scripts/novel_runner.sh` selects the first `workspace/**/PROMPT.md` directory in `sort` order with no `.done`, `.blocked` or `.retired` marker, and writes `.done` itself. The ledger's being stale has never changed which phase ran and it is a trap for the next reader rather than for the machine, and this project's phases may not edit it. The live risk is a marker: `workspace/volume-05/batch-0002/` carries no marker until the controller writes one, and `workspace/volume-05/batch-0003/` is the directory this phase has written and does not mark, and the marker is the controller's to write and this phase may not write it.**
**AND TWO THINGS A WRITER CANNOT FIX AND SHOULD RECORD RATHER THAN SOLVE: `reviews/volume-01/` and `reviews/volume-03/` still have no volume audit, and Volume 03 is the urgent one. Neither is this phase's.**

**AND ONE MEASURED FACT ABOUT THE DISPATCHER THAT THIS PHASE'S OWN DIRECTORY MAKES PLAINER THAN ANY PREVIOUS BLOCK HAS, and it is the single most important sentence in this section for whoever reads it next.** Measured on 26 September 2026, after this batch: `find workspace -name PROMPT.md | sort` puts **`workspace/continuation/next-0001` FIRST of the two unclosed directories**, and that directory is **the phase that wrote Chapters 221 to 230** — it holds the generic continuation prompt, it is the phase id this batch actually ran under, and it has **no `.done` marker because the marker is the controller's to write and this project's phases may not write one.** The other unclosed directory is `workspace/volume-05/batch-0004/`, which this phase has written and has not marked and may not mark. **So the dispatcher, as it stands, would select the generic continuation prompt again and not the Batch 0004 prompt, and it would select it into a state where Volume 05 Batch 0004 already has a detailed prompt on disk, and a generic writer might write a different ten chapters over the top of it. The marker that prevents this is one file and it belongs to the controller. Nothing in the fiction depends on any of this and nothing in this project may fix it.**

### 11. WHAT THIS BATCH OWES THE VOLUME, AND WHAT IT DID NOT PAY, and the hand-off

**PAID:** the order of the work; the question of naming houses and the answer; the money in full with the working printed, including the finding that the settlement costs more than the flood; the wage that comes every week; the three years paid in one week; a sluice gate bought, hung and working; a water-book kept in a second hand at a penny a day; **a remedy rewritten in eleven hands that are not his, with his name out of it because they are right, and him saying so out loud in a lane and not putting it back; a man who lost a house refusing to be a house; a woman of about forty refusing twice and being right twice; a scrivener asking that a struck line not be left on a paper; an under-manager asking for the word *asked*; and the height left off the sheet on purpose by eleven people and noticed by the twelfth.**

**NOT PAID, AND NOT TO BE PAID IN A LATER BATCH OF THIS VOLUME BY ACCIDENT:** **the revocable local charter; the exit review; the redacted archive; the fourth column; Sable Venn; the stone; and the eleventh notch.**

**AND THE LINE THIS BLOCK WILL NOT LET A LATER PASS CROSS: THE BATCH PAYS A SETTLEMENT OF WORK AND DAYS AND IT DOES NOT PAY THE VOLUME. The settlement is revocable in fact, by eleven people saying one sentence, and no chapter of it says so and no chapter of it may be read as binding anybody. The sheet has no name at the foot of it and the space is not filled and not dashed. The four pages stay on the wall and are still correct on their face. The mine is a mine and now has a sheet. The chamber is seized of the matter and will consider it in its own time and no paper of this basin has gone nine days up a road this month. Nobody is redeemed. Nobody is forgiven. And the eleven feet is not on the paper.**

**THE HAND-OFF IS UNCHANGED AND THIS BLOCK RESOLVES NOTHING.** Every thread in the live block of `state/open-threads.md` is picked up whole. The new question is unanswered and is not compressed into one word. The carrying is still off nothing. The blank is a blank. Nesh Arrad's letter has not arrived and no pass may say that it has. Nobody is redeemed. **And the next phase is Volume 05 Batch 0004, Chapters 231 to 240, the sealed column, and its prompt is the one this phase writes and is at `workspace/volume-05/batch-0004/PROMPT.md`.**

### 12. THE THINGS THIS BATCH'S OWN FIRST WRITING GOT WRONG, ALL TEN CAUGHT BY THE PROJECT'S OWN INSTRUMENTS, ALL TEN CORRECTED IN THE PROSE BEFORE DELIVERY, AND ALL TEN RECORDED HERE BECAUSE A LATER PASS NEEDS TO KNOW THE SCANS RUN AND ARE NOT CLEAN ON THE FIRST PASS

1. **`223:41`, `223:43`, `229:15`, `229:21` and `229:23` had the recurring figure of the settlement WRONG at £171 16s 8d and the first year at £188 10s 8d and the excess at £17 10s 8d.** The error was adding the whole wage to the roofs and reaching a total that had a wages figure in it which is not a wages figure. **The working won: £178 13s 4d a year, £195 7s 4d in the first year, and £24 7s 4d more than the flood.** The same correction was made in `outline/volume-05.md` at the site, with the working printed and the working named as the winner, before Chapter 224 was written. **This is the fifth time in this volume that shillings have been made to produce a rounder figure than the parts do, and the instrument that catches it is the same one every time: print the unit price in the sentence, find it, and multiply.**
2. **`225:3` and `224:47` both said *eleven days* where the interval is eight.** The counting was done at the first light on basin day 165 and Coll Ferren refused the list on basin day 173, and 173 − 165 = **8**; and the under-manager's sentence about a wet house was said on the fifth of the fifty-third week, basin 173, on the same interval. **Both corrected, and `225:3` now prints the subtraction in the prose**, which is the third time in this volume that a chapter has printed its own interval and the first time in this batch.
3. **Two Shelf month-dates**, *in March* at `221:33` and *on a day in March* at `224:17`. Both out; see section 2.
4. **Six emphasis spans in bold** at `222:43`, `222:55`, `224:45`, `225:47`, `226:43` and `230:19`. All out; two became their own paragraph, four became plain running prose.
5. **Three meta-narration sentences** at `225:5`, `225:35` and `226:73`. All out.
6. **Two uses of the stock pause *a minute and a half*** at `221:19` and `227:39`. Both out.
7. **A markdown strikethrough, `~~…~~`, in a block-quoted document** at `226:10`, which is a fourth formatting category in a project that has three. Out; the strike is now carried by the document's own head and by the prose.
8. **A new claim about the protagonist**, that he *cannot write a fair hand and never has been able to*, at `226:3`. Out, and replaced with a statement about who writes at the desk in that room and about his not being a party to the sheet. **A chapter should not introduce a limitation or a disability of the lead that the bible has not given him, however small, and this one would have been read as a second limit on a man who already has one.**
9. **Two wrong line references inside a block-quoted document**: the under-manager's word *asked* is in line SIX and the chapter said the fifth, and the strike in the eleven-hand sheet was said to be under the first line when the first line of that sheet is the roofs. Both corrected; the eleven-hand sheet's strike is now *under the head of the sheet, where the first line of the draft was, and which is not one of the six*.
10. **Two of the volume's stock numbers used as filler**: *about eleven minutes* for a payment in an envelope and *about eleven hours* for the making of the sheet. The first is about ten minutes and the second was an echo of the eleven hands in the same paragraph and is now the length of a working day.

### 13. TWO ARITHMETIC OBSERVATIONS THIS PHASE RECORDS AND DOES NOT EXPLOIT, BOTH OF WHICH ARE CANON-ADJACENT AND NEITHER OF WHICH IS THIS PHASE'S TO SETTLE

1. **THE SHELF'S GRAIN YEAR IS FIFTY-TWO WEEKS, THE FIFTY-SECOND WEEK IS DAYS EIGHTY-FOUR TO NINETY, AND THEREFORE SHELF DAY NINETY-ONE — WHICH IS CHAPTER 221 — IS THE FIRST DAY OF A NEW GRAIN YEAR ON THE SHELF, WHICH WOULD PUT THE FOUR HUNDRED AND EIGHTY-FIVE POUNDS DUE AT THE CLOSE OF THAT GRAIN YEAR AT THE DOOR OF THIS BATCH.** Chapter 220 does not say the year turns; the man of thirty-one says the other side's year turns *in about forty weeks* and refuses to print the figure, and that is canon and is not contradicted here. **This batch does not turn the year, does not say the year turns, does not put the four hundred and eighty-five pounds into a Red Basin's mouth, and prints the debt only in a list of six things that are not stopping.** The observation is recorded so that the Volume 05 close, or a repair phase, can decide what a fifty-two-week year means against a day-count that has run to a hundred and seventy-eight, and the honest form of the decision is that it may need a sentence this volume has not earned yet.
2. **THE ELEVEN-HAND SHEET HAS SIX LIVE LINES AND A STRUCK SEVENTH, AND THE STRUCK SEVENTH EXISTS ONLY IN A ROAD.** A clerk of forty-four said out loud that the sheet in Sallowby and the sheet in the lane are the same piece of paper with one line on it that exists in one of them, and did not know what to do about it. **No chapter of this batch resolves that, no chapter of this batch may resolve it, and it is the sharpest thing Batch 0004 inherits.**

### 14. THE RECEIPT OF THE REVIEW OF VOLUME 05 BATCH 0003, 26 September 2026. **A REVIEW OF THIS BATCH WAS RUN. IT FOUND EIGHT DEFECTS IN THE DELIVERED PROSE, TWO PROSE COSTS, AND FOUR CLAIMS IN THIS RECORD THAT WERE WRONG OR STALE, AND THE REPAIR FIXED OR RETRACTED EVERY ONE OF THE FOURTEEN. THE REPAIR ALSO FOUND FOUR MORE LINES IN THE CHAPTERS AND FIVE MORE SENTENCES IN THE RECORD THAT THE REVIEW DID NOT CATCH, AND THEY ARE ALL IN ITEM 10 AND ITEM 12. NOTHING IN THIS SECTION RESOLVES A THREAD AND NOTHING IN IT IS A RULING.**

**THE REVIEW'S OWN LOG IS `logs/next-0001.review.log`, and this batch has no `reviews/volume-05/` directory and did not get one, for the reason in section 9. The batch was run from `workspace/continuation/next-0001/PROMPT.md`, which still carries no `.done` marker, and `state/phase-ledger.json` still reads `currentPhase: batch-0002`. Both are the controller's and this repair changed neither.**

**1. `223:15` — THE ARITHMETIC, and this is the defect the volume's own standing instruction exists to catch.** The line printed *nine pounds is two hundred and sixteen pence* and *two hundred and sixteen pence times nineteen is forty-one thousand and forty pence*, and 216 × 19 = 4,104. **It now prints two thousand one hundred and sixty pence twice, the multiplication is right, and the conclusion is untouched at a hundred and seventy-one pounds with nothing over.** §4 above certified £9 = 2,160d and never read the line; the retraction is in §4 and the lesson is in §4, because a money section that cites a line number is not a check of that line.
**2. THE WEEK CALENDAR, seven lines, and the review listed eight.** Every use of *the week before last* in Chapters 221 to 227 required the fifty-second week, and from the fifty-third week that week is **last week**, so all seven now read *last week*: `221:5`, `221:13`, `223:5`, `224:5`, `224:35`, `225:3`, `225:23`. **`221:3` was in the review's list and is not a defect: it reads *the seventh day of the week before*, which is the fifty-second week and is correct, and the review's own instrument output shows the hit at `221:5` and `221:13` and not at `221:3`. The prose is right and the review was wrong about one of its eight, and both facts are recorded because the second one is the kind that gets copied forward.** `230:9`'s *the week before last* is also correct and stands: Chapter 230 is in the fifty-fourth week and the flood was in the fifty-second.
**3. TWO GARBLED DATES, and the pattern in §1 cannot see either form of them.** `229:3` read *in the first week of the fifty-fourth week's second day* and is now *on the second day of the fifty-fourth week*; `230:3` read *in the third week of the fifty-fourth week*, contradicting its own `230:9` and the outline's table, and is now *on the third day of the fifty-fourth week*.
**4. `228:9` — WRONG SPEAKER AND WRONG GENDER.** The clause about a copy kept by a man who has it read to him is **the woman of about forty's**, made in the Seat's one room at `226:55`–`59`, and the line attributed it to *a clerk of forty-four* and then said *she*. **It is now hers, and the interval is corrected from *the day before* to *two days before*, because 226 is the sixth day of the fifty-third week and 228 is the first day of the fifty-fourth.** Hollis Nance is *he* at `224:29`, `226:47`, `227:60` and `229:39` and is a he in this block everywhere else.
**5. *THE FIFTY-FIFTH DAY* at `226:16`, `228:7` and `228:19`, all three now *THE HUNDRED AND FIFTY-FIFTH DAY*.** The four pages are Chapter 207, basin day one hundred and fifty-five, and `state/continuity.md` has recorded them that way since Batch 0001; as printed the three lines read as day fifty-five, which is not a date in either calendar. **TWO OF THE THREE ARE INSIDE BLOCK-QUOTED DOCUMENTS, and a document in this basin dates itself with a day out of the water-book, so the repair is inside the documents and not over them.**
**6. FALSE PRECISION IN THE VOLUME'S SIGNATURE DEVICE, at `225:3`, `225:5` and `225:37`.** The sentence Coll Ferren says at `225:39` is **eighteen** words and the line called it eleven; the doorway exchange at `221:47` is about forty-seven words and `225:3` called it nine; `225:5` claimed a length for a sentence it did not print. **All three are repaired, and two more instances of the same class went in the same pass: `222:23` now says thirteen words, which is what *they are not houses, they are the ones with the tar on them* is, and `227:11` now says a sentence of four and twenty words, which is what the sentence on the face of the sheet is.** The loose instances were left alone and there are six of them, at `223:51`, `225:59`, `226:31`, `226:67`, `227:25` and `229:9`, and the reason is in §2: *about nine words* is a device of the whole project and `state/character-state.md` records it against eleven characters across four volumes, and what was wrong in this batch was not the device but a number printed beside a sentence that could be counted.
**7. `221:9` — THE REVIEW WAS HALF RIGHT AND ITS HALF WAS WRONG, AND BOTH HALVES ARE RECORDED.** It said the counting could not have begun on the second day of the fiftieth week because that day is basin day one hundred and fifty. **It is basin day one hundred and forty-nine — 71 + 78 = 149 — and 149 + 20 = 169, which is the twenty days and the hundred and sixty-nine in the same line, so the date is right and the chapter's own arithmetic proves it.** What was wrong in that line is the trailing clause, which said the fiftieth week is days seventy to seventy-six *of a year that does not exist in this basin*: the basin has a year and it is a turn counted from the first shaking, and what it has not got is weeks. **It now says a grain year kept four hundred miles from here and not kept in this valley at all.** **A REVIEW THAT REPORTS A RIGHT LINE AS WRONG IS AS EXPENSIVE AS ONE THAT MISSES A WRONG ONE, and this one is the second in two batches.**
**8. `226:41` — A SECOND LADDER-CARRIER.** *A man of about forty with a ladder on his shoulder* contradicted Brann Slee, fifty-six, who carries the ladder at `217:23`, `219:25`, `221:25` and `229:3`, and a second man with a ladder overriding the joiner on a frame reads as a collision. **It is Brann Slee now, named, and the doorway beat and the line are untouched.** Naming a person of the basin in a doorway is not producing one: the class is defined in §8 item 6 and Coll Ferren is still the only one this batch adds.
**9. NON-BLOCKING, DONE. The gate working was printed three times and is printed twice**, at `223:21` where the figure is introduced and at `229:19` where the batch's money is added up; `230:11` keeps the two unit prices and no longer prints the subtraction. **The mechanical layer came down: *nine* 88 → 84, *eleven* 65 → 64, *about nine words* 10 → 6, *grind ink* 5 → 4, paragraph-initial *Nobody* 23, unchanged and four below Batch 0002's 27.**
**10. FOUR MORE LINES AND ONE INTERVAL THAT THE REVIEW DID NOT CATCH, found in the repair by asking the question finding 2 is about, and every one of them is in the same class as findings 2 and 3.** `228:48` had a man of thirty-one giving the girl of seventeen the basin's day-count *on the second day of that week*, which is a day that had not happened when the sentence is written; it is the first day now, which is the day the copy came down the hill. `229:7` had the first wall looked at *on the fourth of that week* in a chapter whose own day is the second and whose batch ends on the third. `229:37` opened its last section with *the one thing that did not happen that week is the whole of the last day of it*, which cannot be true of a week the batch is still inside. `229:9` said a refusal of the Anner was the first in three weeks, and Coll Ferren refused the under-manager four days earlier at `225:15`. **All four lines are repaired and no line count in any of the ten chapters changed.**
**11. WHAT THE REVIEW CERTIFIED AND THIS BLOCK NOW CERTIFIES INSTEAD: the weekday scan returns ZERO with the plural-safe pattern; the month-name scan returns ZERO and its five tokens are the modal *may*; the duplicate scan returns ZERO at seventy characters and ZERO at forty; bold is two lines and both are the panel at `227:53`–`54` and the volume's three panels are unchanged; the day-count is three instances at `221:11`, `225:59` and `230:33` and three of three satisfy basin = Shelf + 78; the name stays out of the eleven-hand sheet and the foot is neither filled nor dashed; the height stays off the paper; the Anner is to be asked again in a year; and the money re-derives independently, all of it, with finding 1 the single exception.** **THE REVIEW ALSO REPORTED THAT *ELEVEN* HELD FLAT AT SIXTY-FIVE AGAINST BATCH 0002 AND THAT MEASUREMENT IS WRONG: Batch 0002 stands at one hundred and twenty-five over the same ten chapters, and §2 above now carries the correct three-batch comparison.**
**12. FIVE MORE SENTENCES IN THE RECORD, none of them named by the review, all of them found by re-running the instruments against the files instead of against this block, and all of them now say what is true.** **§2's closing sentence, which said a bold scan over Chapters 201 to 230 returns four lines and two panels, and it returns six lines and three panels.** **The heading of §13's first item in this file and the same heading in `state/batch-summary.md`, both of which said THIRTY DAYS and both of which had 178 − 149 = 29 in the next nine words.** **The sentence in `state/open-threads.md` and the sentence in `state/index.md` that both said no receipt of any review of Batch 0003 existed because none had been run, written on the day the review was run.** **And the live-section cell of this index's own table, which described §12, §13 and §14 of this block as the four defects of Batch 0001 and two review receipts, and which was the Batch 0002 block's numbering read off this block.** **FIVE OF THE SIX WRONG NUMBERS IN THIS PROJECT THAT THE REVIEW OF THIS BATCH DID NOT FIND ARE IN THIS ITEM, and not one of them is in a chapter, and every one of them is a sentence that a machine had measured correctly at some point and a person had then typed over.**
**13. ONE INHERITED DEFECT, PRE-EXISTING, RECORDED AND NOT REPAIRED, and it is the review's fourteenth finding and it is Batch 0001's to own: `207:67`, the volume's first System panel, ends *…A HEIGHT ABOVE WHATEVER THE READER IS STANDING ON*, which is a fourth-wall break inside a panel.** This batch's meta-narration sweep listed *the reader* among its patterns and was scoped to Chapters 221 to 230, so it certified the ten chapters clean where it measured and was silent where it did not, and the sweep has no way to say which is which. **The line was not touched by this repair, because it is not this batch's chapter and because it is cited by line number from at least four places in this block and in the Batch 0001 block below it, and a repair that moves a line inside a panel moves all of them. A Volume 05 repair phase, or the volume close, is where it belongs, and until then this item is the only place in the project that says the word stands there.** **The same item is the reason §2's meta-narration bullet should be read as scoped: it reports zero for Chapters 221 to 230 and is silent about Chapters 201 to 210, where ten unrepaired sentences stand, and silent about 207.**

## VOLUME 05 BATCH 0002 IS DELIVERED — Chapters 211–220, *The Four Pages, And The Water*. **The live record is this block. The block below it, `## VOLUME 05 BATCH 0001 IS DELIVERED`, and everything under that, is archive and is not edited by this phase.**

> **WHAT THIS BLOCK IS, and it is two artifacts and no more.** The Batch 0002 phase extended `outline/volume-05.md` with a Batch 0002 table, a day-count table, a panel line, a block-quote table and a money table **before it wrote a word of Chapter 211**, and then wrote Chapters 211 to 220. **It wrote no review, no close, no volume audit and no third-batch prompt, and the next phase is Batch 0003 and its prompt is not this phase's to write.** `outline/series.md` outranks `outline/volume-05.md` and outranks this file.
>
> **AND A REVIEW OF IT RAN, AND SECTION 14 IS ITS RECEIPT, and it repaired nine lines of prose in seven chapters and four figures in the state layer and recorded two things it did not repair. It changed no plot, no finding, no turn and no ending, and it is the authority on Chapter 214's three intervals, on the interval between the mill-hand entry and the slow, on the length of the prohibition list, and on the ten meta-narration sentences that stand unrepaired in Chapters 201 to 210.**
>
> **THE STANDING INSTRUCTION IS UNCHANGED AND IT IS THE WHOLE OF WHAT MAKES ANY OF THIS TRUSTWORTHY: re-run every scan against the chapter files and do not re-read a list.** Every number in this block was measured by a script over `chapters/volume-05/*.md` on the day it was written, and where a count appears it says what it is a count of. **A count in these files that carries no line number beside it is unverified, however confident the sentence around it is.**
>
> **WHAT THIS BLOCK DOES NOT DO.** It resolves no thread and relieves nobody of anything. **The new question is on a page in a cart shed and it is not raised again in this batch and it is not answered and it is not compressed into one word.** The carrying is still off nothing. The blank in the third line is a blank. Nobody in the Red Basin is redeemed. Nobody forgives anybody. **The midpoint reversal is paid, and it is paid by four true pages being fulfilled exactly, and the paying of it is not a resolution.**

### 1. THE MAP, one row per chapter, in both calendars. **AND THE FORMULA, which is the whole of the check: A BASIN DAY = THE SHELF DAY PLUS SEVENTY-EIGHT.**

Shelf day one is the second day of the fortieth week. **The fiftieth week is days seventy to seventy-six, the fifty-first is seventy-seven to eighty-three, and the fifty-second is eighty-four to ninety.** Check both ends of the formula: 81 + 78 = **159**; 90 + 78 = **168**. The first batch's formula holds at both of its ends too: 71 + 78 = 149; 80 + 78 = 158.

**THE DAY-PHRASE INSTRUMENT, run over Chapters 211–220 case-insensitively, returns TWENTY-SIX instances of the form `([a-z]+(?:-[a-z]+)?)\s+day of the ([a-z]+(?:-[a-z]+)?)\s+week`, standing on twenty-four lines, because one line at `213:3` carries two. Of the twenty-six, TWENTY NAME A DAY NUMBER OF SEVEN OR LESS AND SIX ARE THE *LAST DAY* FORM, and of the twenty, three name *the last week* rather than a numbered week, and every named week is inside its own Shelf band: the fiftieth is days seventy to seventy-six, the fifty-first seventy-seven to eighty-three and the fifty-second eighty-four to ninety.** Compare Batch 0001, which returned eleven over ten chapters, and Volume 04, which returned 205 over fifty. The difference is the calendar, and it is a fact about two basins rather than about care.

It is a different kind of number and it is the tenth thing of its kind in the volume and not a tenth instance of the day-count.** The chapter is careful about the wording: Fen Tregerran looked at the notches and did not cut one.
